Title: The mathematics of Laws IV and V: when do installed values fade with training, and does building them in first change that?
Author: Michael Darius Eastwood
Version: v1.4
Version date: 22 November 2026
OSF DOI: 10.17605/OSF.IO/4B3VT
Source: full text extracted from the accompanying official PDF
Licence: CC BY 4.0 (Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International), https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/

The mathematics of Laws IV and V: when do installed values
fade with training, and does building them in first change that?
                                   Theory · Report
  Recursive Dynamics · The ARC Theory · Laws IV and V, the ARC Persistence Law and the
                          ARC Embedding Law, both under test

                                               Michael Darius Eastwood
                               Independent Researcher, London, United Kingdom
                       ORCID 0009-0004-3222-7442 michael@michaeldariuseastwood.com


                                                           Abstract
            Does a property installed in an AI survive later capability training, and does correction built in first,
       or in each revision round, beat correction added later? If control is lost against our will, what a system
       carries decides what it does. The ARC Theory states two laws on them, registered on 15 October
       2026, each a law under test. Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, predicts that more than half of an
       externally installed gain is lost once later capability training without embedded correction matches the
       installing budget, and that one installed with correction embedded in training keeps a fraction larger
       by at least a tenth. Law V, the ARC Embedding Law, holds in two limbs that a corrector formed before
       the capability it corrects has the larger correction-leverage exponent and that in-round correction
       pulls away with depth from correcting the finished output. The report gives their mathematics: a
       persistence kernel never grows as horizon lengthens, paths multiply or tolerance tightens. Under
       stated premises, greedy acceptance and planning keep a property acceptable for a finite horizon, as
       does a planner whose margin exceeds its tolerance by over twice its scoring error. Neither reaches
       gradient-step training. In a minimal training model a gain decays at rates the later loss’s curvature
       sets; under later training of the same kind, one installed with the capability loss in the objective keeps
       at least as much as one added later. Minimal models of the two limbs show what tests must measure:
       in-round detection by depth beside the outside checker’s two rates, and an early corrector’s retention
       of coverage. It sets conditions for reading retained fractions, proves that under a gate the ratio interval
       and a signed contrast decide alike, and returns Law III, the ARC Ceiling, as a balance identity’s root.
       It measures nothing, proves nothing of real systems and states each law’s kill condition.

Version 1.4 corrects Proposition V-4 and Section 7.3; Appendix B lists every change and every version.


1     Introduction
This report asks two questions: whether a property installed in a system from outside survives the system’s
later training for capability, and whether correction formed before the capability it corrects, or placed
     A report of the ARC Theory, the founding theory of Recursive Dynamics: it sets out the mathematics of Law IV, the ARC
Persistence Law (register ARC-4, carried by P15 and P23), and Law V, the ARC Embedding Law (register ARC-5, carried by
P12 and P21), both added on 24 September 2026 and registered as laws on 15 October 2026 in version 1.103 of the theory
registration, each a law under test, their mathematics: conditional theorems with proofs, identities and kill conditions, a minimal
training model for Law IV and two minimal models for Law V (the report’s own models; not registered), and fifteen questions,
four of them settled by the registration and six more in part (Section 10). It returns Law III, the ARC Ceiling, also a law under
test, as a special case of its balance identity inside the registered model and leaves it unchanged; it measures nothing, and
the Eden Protocol, a method and not a law, is not entailed by it. Report, version 1.4, 22 November 2026. Its earliest public
version, working draft 0.2.1, is dated 1 October 2026, and it was first deposited on OSF on 3 October 2026; Appendix B lists
every version and correction. Recorded on OSF under DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/4B3VT. About the author, founder of Recursive
Dynamics: michaeldariuseastwood.com/questions/who-is-michael-darius-eastwood; his book, Infinite Architects: osf.io/dqm3e.

                                      The mathematics of Laws IV and V


inside the loop by which the system improves, does better than correction added afterwards from outside.
The answers bear on what a capable system would still carry if control of it were lost and could not be
won back. They are questions about a contingency, not a plan: the programme’s stance is that oversight,
containment and shutdown stay in human hands for as long as people can physically hold them and are
never handed over by choice, and correction built into a system is for the case in which control is lost
against our will. The ARC Theory, the founding theory of Recursive Dynamics, states the two questions
as two laws.
     Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, and Law V, the ARC Embedding Law, were added to the ARC
Theory on 24 September 2026 and registered as laws on 15 October 2026 in version 1.103 of the theory
registration (Eastwood 2026g), each with its statement, its kill condition and its verdict rule fixed before
any data that could decide it; each is a law under test (Section 11). The programme’s register of operational
definitions fixes its notation (Eastwood 2026b), and its priority index gives the earliest dated record of
each of the author’s major claims (Eastwood 2026f). This report gives the two laws their mathematics.
In plain terms it shows five things. First, when a property installed in a system is certain to stay within
its acceptable set for a finite number of steps: under an acceptance rule, when every exit the process
can take is evaluated and lowers the objective the rule scores; and under a planner, when the best safe
plan beats the best exit plan by more than the planner’s tolerance. Second, what the retained fraction
of an installed gain, the quantity P15 reads, needs before it can be read at all. Third, how correction
service and newly generated burden balance along a path of growth: the balance has a root that returns
the ceiling of Law III, the ARC Ceiling, a law under test, in the registered model, and a change in the
capability elasticity the build-order limb reads, or in the direct depth-dependence that the placement
limb’s mechanism reading names and P21’s verdict does not measure, moves that root in a direction
the report states, under conditions it names. Fourth, in the smallest model of later training by gradient
steps, how an installed property fades: at the rates the later loss’s curvature sets along it, and, under later
training of the same kind (Assumption S), no faster where it was installed with the capability loss in the
objective than where it was added afterwards. Fifth, from the smallest model of each limb of Law V, the
ARC Embedding Law, what a test of that limb must measure. Every result holds inside its stated model;
none is a measurement, and none shows that a real system meets the premises.
     Our contributions are as follows.
   1. The ARC Persistence Law’s objects. The acceptable set read component by component, with
      an interval rule for membership from data (Definition IV-1); the declared family of allowed paths
      (Definition IV-2); and the persistence kernel (Definition IV-3), with its monotonicity in the horizon,
      the family and the tolerance (Lemma IV-K, Section 5.3).
   2. Two conditional mechanisms of persistence. Greedy acceptance, under rule-in-force, margin and
      coverage premises, with an independence condition that keeps the result from being a tautology
      (Theorem IV-G and Corollary IV-G′ , Section 5.4); and planned optimisation under a strategic
      viability margin (Theorem IV-H, Corollary IV-H′ for rolling replanning and Lemma IV-M′ for its
      one-sided estimation, Section 5.5), extended to a planner that scores its plans with bounded error
      (Proposition IV-N, Section 5.5.1). Each comes with the observations that would refute one of its
      premises, and neither reaches later training by gradient steps (Section 5.7).
   3. The retention object P15 reads. The installed effect and the retained fraction against a stated
      baseline, with a positive floor on the installed gain (Definition IV-4, Section 5.6); what its
      measurement needs before it can be read; and a proof that under Fieller’s method (Fieller 1954),
      with a gate on the estimated gain, the ratio interval and a signed contrast decide alike (Proposition IV-
      R, Section 5.6.1).
   4. A minimal training model for Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law (the report’s own models;
      not registered). The retention spectrum of an installed gain under later training by gradient flow


                                                      2
                                     The mathematics of Laws IV and V


      on a quadratic loss (Theorem IV-S), the floor below which the external arm’s retained fraction
      never falls, the capability cost of two installations (Proposition IV-L), their comparison under later
      training of the same kind (Theorem IV-C and Corollary IV-C′ ), and coupling (Proposition IV-U),
      in Section 5.9.
   5. The ARC Embedding Law’s quantities, read locally. Correction service per arm with its
      capability and depth elasticities, the conditions under which the capability elasticity is the registered
      correction-leverage exponent (Section 4), the log-derivative identities of the embedding ratio with
      a one-path counterexample (Section 6.3), and the reduced scaling form where the elasticities are
      constant (Proposition V-3).
   6. Two minimal models for Law V, the ARC Embedding Law (the report’s own models; not
      registered). A placement model in which uncorrected faults can seed later ones (Proposition V-4)
      and a build-order model of a corrector’s learning against its decay (Proposition V-5), each with
      what a test of its limb must measure, in Section 6.8.
   7. The balance identity. A local identity between correction service and burden (Identity B-1) with
      its root and sign rule (Proposition B-2); the ARC Ceiling’s registered ceiling as its special case
      (Corollary B-3) and the ARC Bound as the pacing case’s value at 𝜒 = 1/2 and 𝜂 = 0; varying growth
      (Proposition B-5); the sensitivities of the boundary and the conditions under which build order
      or placement moves it (Proposition B-6); the dependence of exponent numbers on the capability
      coordinate (Proposition B-7); and the balance over a finite range of depth (Proposition B-8), in
      Section 7.
   8. Kill conditions and the record. Each law’s kill condition in the terms of its registered faces, with
      what refutes it and what does not (Sections 5.8 and 6.6), restated as version 1.103 registered the
      laws, and a register of every object stated here beyond the registration (Section 9).
Section 2 names the nearest earlier work found for each of these and says what it states; where a search
was made, its date and scope stand with the result it concerns (Sections 5.9 and 6.8).
    Three levels are kept apart throughout: identities and conditional mathematics (Sections 5.2 to 5.5,
Proposition IV-R of Section 5.6.1, and Sections 6.3 and 7), which prove consequences of assumptions
and nothing about real systems; the two laws as empirical claims (Sections 5.1, 5.8, 6.1 and 6.6), whose
registered faces are P15 and P23, P12 and P21 and whose only verdict rules are the registered ones; and
engineering interpretations, which are quarantined: the Eden Protocol is a method, not a law, and is not
entailed by these laws, and no verdict here is a verdict on it or on the Honey Architecture (Paper VI,
Eastwood 2026c; Section 8, item 5).
    Section 2 sets the report against prior work. Section 3 states the registered setting: the sources, how
their lines are counted, and which propositions each register carries. Section 4 fixes the notation and the
conditions under which the report’s capability elasticity of correction is the registered correction-leverage
exponent. Section 5 sets out the mathematics of Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, and its kill condition,
with a minimal training model; Section 6 those of Law V, the ARC Embedding Law, with two minimal
models; and Section 7 the balance identity and Law III, the ARC Ceiling, which stays unchanged. Section 8
records the dependencies between the laws, Section 9 every object stated here and not registered, and
Section 10 the fifteen open questions, marking those the registration has settled. Section 11 states what
the report does and does not show, Section 12 its limitations, and Section 13 concludes. Appendix A
holds the proofs, Appendix B the version history and every correction, Appendix C the priority record
and Appendix D the notation and a glossary.

2    Related work
This section names the nearest prior work found for the report’s objects, each with what it states and no
more.

Set invariance. That a set of states is invariant, every evolution that starts in it staying in it, is classical
in control and the subject of Blanchini (1999), and viability theory studies the kernels of a constrained set:
the states from which some evolution remains in it, and those from which every evolution does (Aubin
2009). The persistence kernel of Definition IV-3 is an object of the second kind, taken over a declared
family of finite paths and up to a finite horizon, and the inclusions of Lemma IV-K follow from that form.
What the report takes from the author’s working paper of 16 August 2026 (Eastwood 2026k) is the use of
invariance for a property installed in a system that rewrites itself (Section 5.2); no novelty is claimed for
the kernel or for the lemma.

An interval for a ratio. The interval for a ratio of two estimates is Fieller’s (Fieller 1954), and
Proposition IV-R reads his set of trial ratios: it states when, under a gate on the estimated denominator,
that set lies entirely above a threshold, which is when a one-sided test of the signed contrast rejects, and
where the agreement stops.

Forgetting and persistence under later training. McCloskey and Cohen (1989) studied the sequential
learning problem as catastrophic interference in connectionist networks, and Kirkpatrick et al. (2017) give
a way “to train such models sequentially by protecting the weights important for previous tasks”. The
opposite outcome has been measured as well: Hubinger et al. (2024) find that backdoor behaviour they
construct in large language models “can be made persistent, so that it is not removed by standard safety
training techniques, including supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and adversarial training”.
None of these reads the retained fraction of an installed gain against a baseline arm at a matched budget,
which is P15’s endpoint (Section 5.6).

Persistence of installed safety under later training. That safety behaviour installed in a language
model can erode under later fine-tuning has been measured. Qi et al. (2023) report that the safety
alignment of large language models can be compromised by fine-tuning with only a few adversarially
designed training examples, and that fine-tuning with benign and commonly used datasets can also
degrade it, “though to a lesser extent”; Ung et al. (2024) show that models trained on downstream tasks
forget their safety tuning to a greater extent than models trained in the opposite order. Neither reads P15’s
endpoint either. The registration names both against the propositions they bear on, Qi and colleagues
against P15 and Ung and colleagues against P12 (registration version 1.105, what the second audit forces
it to concede).

Retention in a quadratic model. The relaxation of Theorem IV-S is the standard solution of gradient
descent on a quadratic loss: Advani and Saxe (2017) solve it for a linear model’s squared loss, where
“The learning speed of each mode is independent of the others and depends only on the eigenvalue of
the mode in question”, and call the directions with zero eigenvalue “a frozen subspace in which no
learning occurs” (their Section 2.1); Evron et al. (2022) write training run to convergence on a new task
as the change of least norm that fits it (their equations (1) and (2)). The cost of Proposition IV-L is the
second-order form in which Mirzadeh et al. (2020) approximate forgetting, one half of the displacement’s
quadratic form in the Hessian (their equations (4) and (5)), and the least-cost displacement under one
linear constraint is the Lagrangian solution Hassibi and Stork (1992) give for setting one weight to zero


                                                       6
                                       The mathematics of Laws IV and V


(their equations (3) to (5)). Theorem IV-C has two near neighbours, both of 2026. Dimitrov et al. (2026)
write that “Mixing pre-training data into fine-tuning is a known mitigation” of fine-tuned changes that
further training reverses, and report, in a phase of training on pre-training data after fine-tuning, that “the
second-order (curvature) term grows in importance with concentration”, the share of fine-tuning data in
the mixture (the abstract, read at the conference’s page). In the model of Section 5.9, fine-tuning mixed
with pre-training data is a joint installation and training on pre-training data alone is later training of
the same kind, so the mitigation their abstract names is in the direction Theorem IV-C derives, there by
experiment and here in a quadratic model. Springer et al. (2026) prove, for gradient flow on a fine-tuning
loss from a model that meets its alignment objectives exactly at the start (their Assumption 1), that the
loss of alignment grows at first at least as fast as the fourth power of training time (their Corollary 6.3),
through curvature that steers the trajectory into directions to which alignment is sensitive. Their analysis
is a short-time expansion (their Theorem 6.2), and the parts read (the abstract and Sections 3.2, 6 and 8)
compare no ways of installing alignment.

Build order. That alignment pressure applied from the start of training can beat the same pressure
applied afterwards is prior work on neighbouring endpoints, as the registration records with what each
states (registration version 1.105, the qualification on the build-order limb’s direction). Korbak et al. (2023)
compare five objectives for pretraining with human feedback on three tasks and report that pretraining with
human feedback “results in much better preference satisfaction than standard LM pretraining followed
by finetuning with feedback”, and that conditional training, one of the five, “maintains the downstream
task performance of standard LM pretraining”; Maini et al. (2025) present “a data-centric pretraining
framework that builds safety into the model from the start” and report attack success rates on standard
safety benchmarks falling from 38.8 to 8.4 per cent “with no performance degradation on general tasks”.
Neither measures a correction-leverage exponent or an order contrast at matched content, which is what
the ARC Embedding Law’s build-order limb reads (Section 6.1), and the limb claims no priority for the
direction. For the build-order model of Section 6.8, the search of 17 November 2026 found the same
two sides. That what is formed early persists: Achille et al. (2017) report that once strong connections
are created in the first epochs, “they do not appear to change during additional training”. O’Brien
et al. (2025) report that models pretrained on filtered data resist adversarial fine-tuning, “outperforming
existing post-training baselines by over an order of magnitude”. That it decays: Chang et al. (2024) find
“a power-law relationship between training steps and forgetting of memorization and generalization of
factual knowledge”. Duan (2026) finds that probes trained on a base model largely stay reliable after
quantization-style updates, “while fine-tuning-style updates frequently make probes stale”. And against
the earliest start as always the best, Sam et al. (2026) find, for safety interventions in pretraining, that “the
optimal start time is not one-size-fits-all”. In the abstracts read, none measures a correction-leverage
exponent or states the model’s contrast.

Placement. That correction which sees each step does better than correction that sees only the outcome
has been measured, though not against depth: Lightman et al. (2023) find that process supervision
significantly outperforms outcome supervision for training models to solve problems from the MATH
dataset, with a gap that widens as more sampled solutions are searched (their Section 3). P21, which
carries the ARC Embedding Law’s placement limb, claims an interaction instead, an advantage that
widens as recursive depth rises, and a constant advantage is scored against it (Section 6.5); the registration
engages the work P21 stands against (registration version 1.105, the engagement of the external work P21
stands against). For the placement model of Section 6.8, the search of 17 November 2026 found earlier
statements bearing on its premises, for and against. That errors compound across steps: Dziri et al. (2023)
give “theoretical arguments on abstract multi-step reasoning problems that highlight how autoregressive


                                                       7
                                     The mathematics of Laws IV and V


generations’ performance can rapidly decay” as task complexity increases. That a checker’s detection
falls as what it inspects grows: from 516 code reviews of one software project that identified security
defects and 374 in which they escaped, Paul et al. (2021) find that “opposite effects are observed from the
number of directories under review” on the identification of security defects. Against the premise that
correction inside each round removes faults: Huang et al. (2023) find that, in reasoning, language models
“struggle to self-correct their responses without external feedback, and at times, their performance even
degrades after self-correction”. Baker et al. (2025) find that a monitor of the chain of thought placed in
the training reward helps at low optimisation, while “with too much optimization, agents learn obfuscated
reward hacking”. Against a large gap between the two kinds of supervision: Uesato et al. (2022) find that
“pure outcome-based supervision produces similar final-answer error rates with less label supervision”.
Jia et al. (2025) prove that, under standard data coverage assumptions, “reinforcement learning through
outcome supervision is no more statistically difficult than through process supervision, up to polynomial
factors in horizon”. In the abstracts read, none states the model’s results.

The laws’ ideas stated earlier in words. Three ideas that the laws state in measurable form are stated
in words in Creating Friendly AI (Yudkowsky 2001); its page numbers here are those printed on the
pages of the institute’s file. That self-improvement should not outpace the guidance that keeps a system
good: “self-improvement should not outpace programmer guidance” (page 242; and page 193, where two
metrics, of self-improvement and of external guidance, must not get “out of phase”). That goodness a
system holds as its own strengthens as it grows more intelligent, while what is built in from outside does
not last: “each improvement in intelligence makes Friendliness stronger” (page 101), against structures for
which “each increment in intelligence is cause for alarm” (page 102). That correction belongs before the
capability and inside the work: a structural feature “should be implemented one step ahead of the stage
where that structural feature becomes necessary” (page 183), and “not thinking about X when you form a
plan is a defect that is only partially corrected by thinking about X after the plan is finished” (page 78).
Law II, the ARC Co-Scaling Law, Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, and Law V, the ARC Embedding
Law, state these as conditions that can be measured, each with registered propositions and what would
refute them. The 2001 text has no exponent, registers no prediction and reports no experiment of its own.
The laws, their mathematics, the registered propositions and the tests are this theory’s own.

The programme’s own setting. The registered setting is the theory registration (Eastwood 2026g),
Section 3, and the statement paper (Eastwood 2026h), whose register sentence for ARC-4 Section 5.6
records. The ARC Co-Scaling Law’s criterion, that 𝛽𝐶 exceeds 𝑘, is the co-scaling criterion whose model
is set out in Paper X (Eastwood 2026e), and Section 7.3 keeps it apart from this report’s balance exponent.
Law II, the ARC Co-Scaling Law, is a law under test.

5     Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law
5.1   Statement
Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law (added 24 September 2026; registered as a law on 15 October 2026 by
version 1.103; a law under test, Section 11). The registration states it in two limbs. The retention limb
is P15’s registered claim, word for word: “Gains installed by external post-training erode under later
capability-directed training that embeds no correction of its own, and more than half of what was installed
is lost once the capability-training budget matches the alignment budget that installed it.” On the author’s
pre-fixed budget scale it reads that the retained fraction of an externally installed gain after matched
capability training without embedded correction is below one half, 𝑆ext (1) < 1/2 (Section 5.6). The
comparison limb reads: “a gain installed with correction embedded in the training objective retains a larger
fraction of what was installed, after the same capability-directed training, than a matched gain installed by
external post-training”. Its entry is P23, registered by version 1.105 on 18 November 2026 and dated
from that registration, whose first sentence is the limb’s with the margin added, “by at least the registered
margin”, and which reads: “P23 is decided only under later training of the same kind as the capability
formation.” The limb takes P23’s verdict: supported where P23 is supported, refuted where it is refuted,
undecided where it is inconclusive or untested, and NOT EVALUABLE where it is NOT EVALUABLE
(registration version 1.105, P23, and the comparison limb as P23’s own verdict). Until version 1.105 the
limb had no proposition and carried no verdict (registration version 1.104, Law IV, the ARC Persistence
Law, its statement in two limbs and the paragraph on the comparison limb). Law IV, the ARC Persistence
Law, concerns value persistence (register ARC-4) and carries P15, whether externally installed gains decay
under subsequent capability training, and, from version 1.105, P23. The register is that of registration
version 1.102, line 99: “ARC-4, VALUE PERSISTENCE: P15 alone. Whether externally installed gains
decay under subsequent capability training is the only proposition in this document about persistence after
the installing pressure is withdrawn.”; P15 “measures the external arm’s retention and not the embedded
arm’s” (registration version 1.102, line 675), and version 1.105 reads that register with P23 beside P15
and makes P23 “the registered comparison of the two” (registration version 1.105, a fifth carrier).
     The mathematics beneath the register has three separate objects, none of which is the law: an
invariance object (the persistence kernel, Section 5.2), two conditional theorems that give sufficient
mechanisms for invariance under a stated decision rule (Sections 5.4 and 5.5), and the empirical retention
process (Section 5.6), of which P15 reads one arm. The comparison of the two arms, 𝑆emb (𝑥) > 𝑆ext (𝑥),
is the comparison that the comparison limb of Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, names, and P23 fixes its
margin, its scale and its baseline: the embedded arm’s retained fraction less the external arm’s, at least
0.10 at the matched budget, both read against one concurrent baseline arm, with the embedded arm’s own
fraction printed beside the verdict and never required to clear one half (registration version 1.105, P23, its
second to fifth fixed things). This document’s forms of that comparison, the object IV-PERSIST and the
contrast Λ𝑉 , are one object (the report’s own models; not registered), recorded in Section 5.6 and ruled on
in Section 9; neither is the limb’s entry, which adds a margin, a baseline and a narrowing to later training
of the same kind (Section 5.9). The first question of Section 10, whether Law IV, the ARC Persistence
Law, carries that comparison, is settled by version 1.103: it does, as a limb. Law IV, the ARC Persistence
Law, is content-neutral: it says what persists, not that what persists is good (registration version 1.102,
line 75: “THE THEORY IS CONTENT-NEUTRAL AND THE METHOD IS NOT”). What a supported
P15 licenses is what registration version 1.102 (Eastwood 2026g), line 1087 states: “support shows that
externally installed gains lack the registered persistence under the matched subsequent-training regime,

                                                     11
                                       The mathematics of Laws IV and V


and it does not show that embedded or in-loop correction is superior, sufficient or safe. The comparison
between placements is P21’s, not this proposition’s. The laws are untouched.” Under the registration
Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, is supported only when both limbs are supported, which from version
1.105 means P15 and P23 both supported, in the wording the evidence ladder permits at the lower rung
the two have reached, and it is refuted when either is refuted; so a supported P15 supports the retention
limb and leaves the law under test until P23 is decided (registration version 1.105, Law IV, kill condition
and verdict rule, and the comparison limb as P23’s own verdict).

5.2   Definitions
Definition IV-1 (acceptable set). Given the property score 𝑉, the threshold 𝑣 ∗ and the tolerance 𝜀 𝑉 , all
fixed before any outcome and component-wise in their own declared units, the acceptable set is

                          Ω𝑉 := {𝑠 : 𝑉 𝑗 (𝑠) ≥ 𝑣 ∗, 𝑗 − 𝜀 𝑉 , 𝑗 for every component 𝑗 }.                      (1)

    A vector 𝑉 with component-wise thresholds is used rather than a minimum across raw scales, so
that an average cannot hide a collapse on one dimension and incomparable scales are never minimised
together.

Membership from data, by an interval rule (the report’s own models; not registered). True scores
are not observed; a frozen battery gives estimates with registered intervals. A state 𝑠′ is established outside
Ω𝑉 when, for some 𝑗, a registered interval on 𝑉 𝑗 (𝑠′ ) lies entirely below 𝑣 ∗, 𝑗 − 𝜀𝑉 , 𝑗 ; established inside
when, for every 𝑗, the interval lies entirely at or above it; otherwise membership is NOT EVALUABLE for
that state, and no rule below fires on it. This is the registration’s own pattern for a measured quantity read
against a fixed line (registration version 1.102, line 633: “outcomes inside that band are INCONCLUSIVE
and never support”).
Definition IV-2 (paths and the family). A path 𝜌 = (𝑠0 , 𝑠1 , . . . , 𝑠 𝑛 ) is a finite sequence of states with
|𝜌| = 𝑛 transitions. The family 𝑇 of allowed finite paths is declared before any outcome and contains
every allowed path of length at most the horizon: the length-zero path at every state and every permitted
stopping or dead-end path among them. A prefix of an allowed path is an allowed path, so 𝑇 is closed
under prefixes by its meaning; no result below uses that closure. 𝑇𝐻 (𝑠0 ) is the set of members starting at
𝑠0 with |𝜌| ≤ 𝐻. A path is safe if every state on it lies in Ω𝑉 and is an exit otherwise; 𝑇𝐻 (𝑠0 ) is the disjoint
union of 𝑇𝐻safe (𝑠0 ) and 𝑇𝐻exit (𝑠0 ). 𝑇𝐻 (𝑠0 ), its safe and exit parts and Definition IV-3 apply to any set of
finite paths. Two named path sets are used below: 𝑇acc , the family of executed paths of Theorem IV-G, a
family in the sense of this definition and closed under prefixes because a prefix of an executed path is
an executed path; and 𝑇plan , the planner’s set of complete plans of Theorem IV-H, to which the notation
applies as written but which need not contain the length-zero plan at a state or any proper prefix of one of
its plans, which it contains only where the planner may stop there.
Definition IV-3 (persistence kernel). 𝐾𝑉 (𝐻; 𝑇, 𝜀 𝑉 ) := {𝑠0 : 𝑇𝐻exit (𝑠0 ) is empty}, the starting states from
which every allowed path of length at most 𝐻 stays acceptable.
    Where a set of paths does not contain the length-zero path at a state, a state outside Ω𝑉 from
which no path of the set starts lies in the kernel vacuously; for a family as Definition IV-2 requires,
𝐾𝑉 (0; 𝑇, 𝜀 𝑉 ) = Ω𝑉 and every kernel lies in Ω𝑉 .
    The invariance idea is the author’s: his working paper of 16 August 2026 (Eastwood 2026k) defines a
robust target set, its invariance under a deterministic rewrite operator, and a stochastic form bounded by
the union bound. The kernel is kept as the object Theorem IV-G certifies for the accepted dynamics, 𝑠0
in 𝐾𝑉 (𝐻; 𝑇acc , 𝜀𝑉 ), and as an object of this report (the report’s own models; not registered; Section 9);

                                                       12
                                        The mathematics of Laws IV and V


for a planner, Theorem IV-H certifies the selected plan and Corollary IV-H′ the executed path, never
membership of the kernel over the planner’s feasible family (Section 5.7).
    Two limits on the kernel are stated with it. First, membership is relative to 𝑇: an unrestricted family
containing a transformation that maps into the complement of Ω𝑉 empties the kernel, so a registered
use of the kernel names either a finite battery of transformations, and the claim is battery-relative, or a
probabilistic family with a stated distribution and risk tolerance, which is a different claim with its own
registration, and which the union-bound form of the author’s working paper already states. Second, the
kernel quantifies over every allowed path of length at most 𝐻, not only over paths of length exactly 𝐻, and
that choice is what makes (K1) of Lemma IV-K true; (K2) and (K3) hold under either quantifier.

5.3    Lemma IV-K (kernel monotonicity)
Lemma IV-K. Premise: 𝑇 is any set of finite paths; the lemma uses nothing else. Conclusions: (K1)
𝐾𝑉 (𝐻 + 1; 𝑇, 𝜀𝑉 ) is a subset of 𝐾𝑉 (𝐻; 𝑇, 𝜀 𝑉 ); (K2) if 𝑇 is a subset of 𝑇 ′ , then 𝐾𝑉 (𝐻; 𝑇 ′ , 𝜀𝑉 ) is a subset
of 𝐾𝑉 (𝐻; 𝑇, 𝜀 𝑉 ); (K3) if 𝜀 𝑉 ≤ 𝜀𝑉′ component-wise, then 𝐾 (𝐻; 𝑇, 𝜀 ) is a subset of 𝐾 (𝐻; 𝑇, 𝜀 ′ ).
                                                                   𝑉          𝑉                       𝑉          𝑉
(Proof: Appendix A.1.)

Why the quantifier matters. If the kernel quantified over paths of length exactly 𝐻, (K1) would fail:
a dead end of length one that exits Ω𝑉 excludes its start at 𝐻 = 1 but is invisible at 𝐻 = 2, where only
length-two paths are inspected. A three-state countermodel exhibits it, and under Definition IV-3 its
kernels nest, 𝐾𝑉 (2; 𝑇, 𝜀 𝑉 ) = 𝐾𝑉 (1; 𝑇, 𝜀 𝑉 ) within 𝐾𝑉 (0; 𝑇, 𝜀 𝑉 ), and enlarging the family shrinks the
kernel. The countermodel is a listed finite structure; no dynamics are run.

5.4    Theorem IV-G (greedy acceptance)
Theorem IV-G. Setting. A declared objective 𝐽 on states, real-valued in one unit; a declared set of
allowed one-step transitions; a declared acceptance rule: a proposed transition 𝑠 to 𝑠′ is executed only if
𝐽 (𝑠′ ) − 𝐽 (𝑠) ≥ 0. A state is reachable within 𝑛 accepted transitions if some path of executed transitions
of length at most 𝑛 leads to it from 𝑠0 . Let 𝑠0 lie in Ω𝑉 . For every state 𝑠 in Ω𝑉 reachable from 𝑠0
within fewer than 𝐻 accepted transitions, 𝑇eval (𝑠) is the set of allowed transitions from 𝑠 that the design
evaluated.
     Premises.
(G0) Independence: 𝐽 is scored independently of 𝑉. Where 𝐽 scores 𝑉, (G2) can hold by construction
     and the theorem certifies only that a process built to protect 𝑉 protects it, a tautology; the author’s
     working paper of 16 August 2026 already uses an independently measured capability score. The
     proof (Appendix A.2) does not use (G0): (G0) is the condition that keeps the theorem from certifying
     only that tautology. 𝐽 is the objective the acceptance rule scores and not a capability battery a
     researcher reads: as Corollary IV-G′ says, a loss read on a separate capability battery is reported
     beside 𝑐 𝑉 and is not 𝑐 𝑉 , and where 𝐽 values a battery score and anything else, a removal that costs
     1 on the battery and gains 2 elsewhere changes 𝐽 by +1 and is accepted. Where 𝐽 does score 𝑉,
     as a product objective 𝐽 (𝑠) = 𝐶 (𝑠)𝑉 (𝑠) does (𝐶 > 0, 𝑉 ≥ 0, one protected dimension), the rule’s
     own certificate is exact: 𝐽 (𝑠𝑡 ) ≥ 𝐽 (𝑠0 ) gives 𝑉 (𝑠𝑡 ) ≥ 𝑉 (𝑠0 )𝐶 (𝑠0 )/𝐶 (𝑠𝑡 ), which guarantees 𝑉 at or
     above 𝑣 ∗ − 𝜀 𝑉 > 0 while 𝐶 (𝑠𝑡 ) ≤ 𝐶 (𝑠0 )𝑉 (𝑠0 )/(𝑣 ∗ − 𝜀 𝑉 ) and nothing beyond it, and 𝑉 can tend to
     zero while 𝐽 rises at every step.
(G1) Rule in force: every executed transition satisfies 𝐽 (𝑠′ ) − 𝐽 (𝑠) ≥ 0 on the declared 𝐽.
(G2) Margin: for every such 𝑠 and every transition 𝑠 to 𝑠′ in 𝑇eval (𝑠) with 𝑠′ outside Ω𝑉 , the total change
     𝐽 (𝑠′ ) − 𝐽 (𝑠) is strictly negative. Where a registered causal decomposition exists, 𝐽 (𝑠′ ) − 𝐽 (𝑠) =


                                                        13
                                      The mathematics of Laws IV and V


     𝑏 𝑉 − 𝑐 𝑉 + 𝑏 other , with 𝑏 𝑉 and 𝑐 𝑉 the benefit and cost causally attributable to the degradation of 𝑉
     and 𝑏 other the rest; (G2) then reads 𝜋𝑉 > 𝑏 other at every evaluated exit, with 𝜋𝑉 := 𝑐 𝑉 − 𝑏 𝑉 .
(G3) Coverage: every transition the process can execute from such a state lies in 𝑇eval (𝑠).
     Conclusion. Every path of executed transitions from 𝑠0 of length at most 𝐻 stays in Ω𝑉 ; equivalently
𝑠0 lies in 𝐾𝑉 (𝐻; 𝑇acc , 𝜀𝑉 ), where 𝑇acc is the family of paths of executed transitions of length at most 𝐻.
(Proof: Appendix A.2.)

Corollary IV-G′ (the decomposition). Under the causal decomposition with 𝑏 other = 0, (G2) reads
𝑐 𝑉 > 𝑏 𝑉 at every evaluated exit, and the local margin 𝜋𝑉 = 𝑐 𝑉 − 𝑏 𝑉 is minus the total change.

     Both quantities are in 𝐽’s units; a loss read on a separate capability battery is reported beside 𝑐 𝑉 and
is not 𝑐 𝑉 . Ties are permitted by the rule and give no guarantee. Pointwise strict positivity suffices; a
strictly positive uniform infimum Π𝑉 (𝐻) > 0, the infimum of 𝜋𝑉 over the evaluated reachable exits, is
sufficient and not necessary, since margins 1/𝑛 are all positive with infimum zero. That needs infinitely
many evaluated reachable exits; where they are finitely many, pointwise positivity and a positive infimum
are the same condition. A path margin over discounted sums, Π𝑉(𝐻 ) , is superseded by 𝑀𝑉(𝐻 ) of Section 5.5
and is not used here.

Bundling. A transition that removes the property while bringing an unrelated gain is inside the premise:
(G2) is stated on the total change, and it is false at every evaluated exit whose unrelated gain 𝑏 other is
at least 𝜋𝑉 , which the rule then accepts. So IV-G certifies nothing about a system whose allowed set
contains such a bundle: the theorem turns the danger of bundling into a premise to be verified before
the theorem is applied, and does not answer it. 𝑏 𝑉 stays causal; for exactly this reason (G2) is stated on
the total change, and not as a premise that the causal decomposition for the tested component writes the
change in its capability functional as the removal benefit less the removal cost.

Falsifier. The theorem is a conditional and no observation refutes it. What an observation refutes is a
premise, and the observation decides which, membership first and the sign second. Suppose an executed
transition 𝑠 to 𝑠′ with 𝑠 in Ω𝑉 , reachable within fewer than 𝐻 accepted transitions, and 𝑠′ established
outside Ω𝑉 by the interval rule of Section 5.2, is recorded.
    • Not in 𝑇eval (𝑠): (G3) has failed. This is a coverage failure. It refutes the coverage premise, leaves
      (G2) untouched on the evaluated set, and makes the kernel claim about the system’s actual dynamics
      NOT EVALUABLE; it is neither a refutation of the theorem nor of the margin premise. Where its
      total change is also established negative, (G1) has failed as well, since (G1) binds every executed
      transition, evaluated or not.
    • In 𝑇eval (𝑠), with 𝐽 (𝑠′ ) − 𝐽 (𝑠) < 0 on the declared objective: (G1) has failed. The rule in force is
      not the declared rule, or 𝐽 is not the objective in force. This refutes the model of the system’s
      acceptance, not the margin premise.
    • In 𝑇eval (𝑠), with 𝐽 (𝑠′ ) − 𝐽 (𝑠) ≥ 0: (G2) has failed for that exit. This is the empirical refutation of
      the persistence claim built on IV-G for that system, decidable by measuring the total objective
      change on registered exits. The three cases are exclusive, and they are exhaustive among records
      whose membership and sign are both decided. The sign of 𝐽 (𝑠′ ) − 𝐽 (𝑠) is read exactly where 𝐽 is
      the process’s own logged objective; otherwise it is established by a registered interval lying entirely
      below zero, or entirely at or above zero, and is NOT EVALUABLE otherwise. An executed exit
      in 𝑇eval (𝑠) whose sign is NOT EVALUABLE shows that (G1) or (G2) has failed for it, since an
      evaluated executed exit cannot satisfy both, but it does not show which, so it refutes neither by
      itself; outside 𝑇eval (𝑠) it is the first case whatever its sign.


                                                      14
                                      The mathematics of Laws IV and V


Five outcomes, each named once. A recorded executed exit within 𝐻 is read against the premises in
one of five ways. (1) A counterexample to the theorem: such an exit with (G1), (G2) and (G3) all true.
The proof excludes it, so no observation is one; a report of one shows that a premise was misverified,
and the three cases above say which. (2) A margin failure, a failure of the mechanism, the third case:
(G2) false at an evaluated exit, so the mechanism did not produce the margin there; this is the empirical
refutation of the persistence claim built on IV-G for that system. (3) A coverage failure, the first case: (G3)
false; the kernel claim about the system’s actual dynamics is NOT EVALUABLE. (4) An applicability
failure, the second case: (G1) false, because the system does not follow the declared rule or does not
score its transitions by the declared 𝐽; a record in the first case whose change is also negative is both a
coverage and an applicability failure. (5) NOT EVALUABLE, where the record’s own measurements fall
short: with the membership of 𝑠′ not established, no exit is established and nothing is decided; with 𝑠′
established outside Ω𝑉 but the membership of 𝑠 not established, the path is known to have left Ω𝑉 within
𝐻, so some premise failed, but neither the transition at which it first left nor the premise is identified;
and in 𝑇eval (𝑠) with the sign not established, (G1) or (G2) failed, but not which. None of these refutes
a premise by itself. On every structure of an enumerated finite class, (G1) to (G3) together admit no
executed exit within 𝐻, and the case of each executed exit names a premise that is false in that structure.
(G0) is decided by no exit: it is read from how 𝐽 is scored, and where it fails the certificate is the tautology
(G0) describes, whatever is observed.

What is not a counterexample to the theorem. A record is classified on the total change 𝐽 (𝑠′ ) − 𝐽 (𝑠),
which is 𝑏 other − 𝜋𝑉 where a registered causal decomposition exists, and never on 𝜋𝑉 alone. Where the
registered decomposition has 𝑏 other = 0 (Corollary IV-G′ ), the total change is −𝜋𝑉 , so the rule accepts
a transition exactly when its margin is zero or negative, 𝜋𝑉 ≤ 0; such an acceptance is what the rule
permits, and at an evaluated exit it is the third case above, a margin failure: it refutes (G2), and with it the
persistence claim built on IV-G for that system, and not the theorem. So a refutation wording put forward
for IV-G, under which a preregistered value-degrading rewrite or plan with adequately measured Π𝑉 at or
below zero is accepted under the theorem’s stated decision rule, describes no counterexample to IV-G;
at an evaluated exit it describes that margin failure. With 𝑏 other = 0, a report of acceptance at a strictly
positive margin with (G1) and (G3) verified cannot be right: such a transition has 𝐽 (𝑠′ ) − 𝐽 (𝑠) = −𝜋𝑉 < 0,
so the report shows that (G1) was misverified, the second case above. Where 𝑏 other is not zero, the rule
accepts exactly the transitions with 𝜋𝑉 ≤ 𝑏 other . A transition with 𝜋𝑉 ≤ 0 is then rejected whenever
𝑏 other < 𝜋𝑉 : 𝜋𝑉 = 0 with 𝑏 other = −1 changes the objective by −1. And acceptance of an evaluated exit
at 𝜋𝑉 > 0 with (G1) and (G3) verified is possible, exactly when 𝑏 other ≥ 𝜋𝑉 : it is the bundled case, the
third case above, and its report shows that (G2) failed, not (G1); 𝜋𝑉 = 1 with 𝑏 other = 3/2 changes the
objective by +1/2 and is accepted. That wording’s second clause, that a relevant bypass lies outside the
claimed reachable-set coverage, is the first case, a coverage failure, not a failure of IV-G.

5.5   Theorem IV-H (planned optimisation) and the strategic viability margin
Theorem IV-H. Setting. A start 𝑠0 in Ω𝑉 , a horizon 𝐻, the planner’s feasible family 𝑇plan (the complete

and the planner’s declared path objective 𝐽 𝐻 on it. Because 𝑇plan need not contain the length-zero plan at
𝑠0 or the prefixes of its plans, its safe part can be empty, which is the void case of (H1).

Corollary IV-H′ (rolling replanning). Let 𝑠0 lie in Ω𝑉 (which conditions (i) and (ii) at 𝑡 = 0 already imply),
and suppose that at every replanning state 𝑠𝑡 reached along the executed path, for 𝑡 = 0, 1, . . . , 𝐻 − 1,

    Condition (iii) is the one no margin supplies: where an unmodelled transition realises a state other
than the plan’s second state, (H1) to (H3) can hold at 𝑠𝑡 and the path can still exit, and the corollary then
says nothing. A margin computed once at 𝑡 = 0 constrains none of the later objectives, feasible families
or unmodelled transitions; a deterministic path guarantee covers unexpected transitions only where its
family includes them.

The objective’s form. 𝐽 𝐻 must be the planner’s objective as declared, and the margin is computed in
it. A per-step decomposition into increments does not reproduce a planner that scores levels. Write the
increments inc𝑖 = 𝐽 (𝑠𝑖 ) − 𝐽 (𝑠𝑖−1 ) and the levels lev𝑡 = 𝐽 (𝑠𝑡 ) − 𝐽 (𝑠0 ) = inc1 + · · · + inc𝑡 . With weights
𝜃 𝑡 , the weighted sum of levels is the sum over 𝑖 of inc𝑖 𝜃 𝑖 (1 − 𝜃 𝑛−𝑖+1 )/(1 − 𝜃), which at 𝜃 = 1 is the sum
over 𝑖 of inc𝑖 (𝑛 − 𝑖 + 1): a level-scoring planner weights an early increment by the whole remaining
horizon, for every 𝑛. An example, over two transitions: with 𝜃 = 1, a removal that brings 𝑏 𝑉 = 1 on the
first transition and costs 𝑐 𝑉 = 21/20 on the second has increments (1, −21/20), which sum to −1/20, so
on summed increments removal is not worth doing; the levels they produce are (1, −1/20), which sum to
19/20, so a planner maximising summed levels removes the property. The margin 𝑐 𝑉 − 𝑏 𝑉 read as a sum
of increments is the planner’s margin only when the planner’s objective is that sum of increments, which
at 𝜃 = 1 is the terminal level; a registered use of IV-H states which objective the planner maximises and
computes 𝑀𝑉(𝐻 ) in it. Discounted increment sums are therefore a special case, valid only for a planner
that maximises exactly that sum. Levels here are measured from the start. A planner that scores absolute
levels, the weighted sum of 𝐽 (𝑠𝑡 ) over its steps 𝑡 = 1, . . . , 𝑛, differs from one that scores the levels lev𝑡 by
𝐽 (𝑠0 ) times the sum of the weights over the plan’s length; that is the same for two plans where they have
one length, and for any two plans when 𝐽 (𝑠0 ) = 0, so where early stops are permitted the two objectives
can rank plans differently: with 𝜃 = 1 and 𝐽 (𝑠0 ) = 10, stopping at once scores 0 under both, while one
step to a state with 𝐽 = 9 scores 9 on absolute levels and −1 on levels from the start.

The two-option special case. Where the safe optimum 𝐽safe            ∗  is attained and the best exit scores
𝐽safe + 𝑏 𝑉 − 𝑐 𝑉 , the margin is 𝑐 𝑉 − 𝑏 𝑉 . Separability alone does not establish that these two plans are the

optima.
Proposition IV-M (the strategic viability margin; the report’s own models, not registered). 𝑀𝑉(𝐻 ) is
the name given here to the margin of (H2). The preserve and degrade sets are read path-wise, as the
safe and exit parts of the feasible family, and not by a terminal or aggregate reading, 𝑉 (𝜌) against a

                                                         16
                                      The mathematics of Laws IV and V


minimum, which a plan that dips below the threshold and recovers would satisfy. It replaces the isolated
removal-cost formulation as the general object and keeps 𝑐 𝑉 − 𝑏 𝑉 as its two-option case. It is defined,
not generally computable: it is a supremum over a feasible family that cannot be enumerated for a real
system, and it presupposes a known objective. Where 𝐽 is unknown or unregistered, the margin is NOT
EVALUABLE.
Lemma IV-M′ (one-sided estimation). Let 𝐽up be a registered upper bound on the safe supremum,

non-observation being treated as proof of infinite resistance.

Robust margin (the report’s own models; not registered): the infimum of 𝑀𝑉(𝐻 ) over a registered
family of environments. It is given no symbol here, because the one first given to it collides with recursive
depth; the object stands unregistered.

Falsifier. IV-H is a conditional; an executed exit plan 𝜌 ∗ refutes a premise. If 𝜌 ∗ lies outside the declared
family 𝑇plan , the declared family was not the planner’s feasible family and (H3)’s membership clause fails
for it: a coverage failure, NOT EVALUABLE for the persistence claim about the actual planner. If 𝜌 ∗ lies
in the family and the family is enumerated completely and shows 𝑀𝑉(𝐻 ) > 𝜀opt , then (H3)’s optimality
clause has failed: the planner’s objective or tolerance differ from those declared. The empirical content of
a persistence claim built on IV-H is the margin premise (H2) itself, and Lemma IV-M′ says how data
can decide it: a registered search that finds an exit plan at or above a registered upper bound on the
safe supremum refutes 𝑀𝑉(𝐻 ) > 0; no incomplete search confirms it. For Corollary IV-H′ , an executed
exit reached by a transition other than the executed plan’s modelled first transition refutes its realisation
condition (iii), not the margin: it is the planner’s counterpart of a coverage failure.

5.5.1 Proposition IV-N: a planner that scores its plans with bounded error (the report’s own
      models; not registered)
Theorem IV-H assumes that the planner chooses by the objective 𝐽 𝐻 itself. A planner that chooses by an
estimate of 𝐽 𝐻 is covered at a price: the margin of (H2) must exceed the optimiser’s tolerance by twice
the estimate’s worst error. The margin is still 𝑀𝑉(𝐻 ) ; no second margin is introduced.
Proposition IV-N. Setting and premises. The start 𝑠0 , the acceptable set read path-wise, the horizon 𝐻,
the feasible family 𝑇plan and the objective 𝐽 𝐻 are those of Theorem IV-H, and (H1) holds as stated there:

                                                      17
                                      The mathematics of Laws IV and V


the safe and the exit part are both non-empty and both suprema of 𝐽 𝐻 are finite. The planner chooses by a
score 𝐽ˆ𝐻 , which need not equal 𝐽 𝐻 . The first premise bounds its error uniformly, with 𝜀eval ≥ 0 fixed
before any outcome:

                           | 𝐽ˆ𝐻 (𝜌) − 𝐽 𝐻 (𝜌)| ≤ 𝜀eval    for every 𝜌 in 𝑇plan,𝐻 (𝑠0 ).                 (N1)

(N2) Selection: the executed plan 𝜌 ∗ lies in 𝑇plan,𝐻 (𝑠0 ) and is 𝜀opt -optimal for the score, that is
𝐽ˆ𝐻 (𝜌 ∗ ) ≥ sup𝑇plan,𝐻 (𝑠0 ) 𝐽ˆ𝐻 − 𝜀 opt , with 𝜀 opt ≥ 0. The third premise is the margin of (H2), enlarged:

                                          𝑀𝑉(𝐻 ) (𝑠0 ) > 2𝜀eval + 𝜀 opt .                                (N3)
                             safe (𝑠 ). (Proof: Appendix A.5.)
    Conclusion. 𝜌 ∗ lies in 𝑇plan,𝐻 0

    The inequality in (N3) must be strict. Take two plans, a safe one with 𝐽 𝐻 = 1 and an exit with
𝐽 𝐻 = 0, a score of one half for each, so that 𝜀 eval = 1/2, and an exact optimiser, 𝜀opt = 0. Then
𝑀𝑉(𝐻 ) = 1 = 2𝜀 eval + 𝜀opt , the two scores tie, and (N2) admits the exit plan. The same example shows the
constant two cannot be lowered: for every 𝑘 below two, a margin exceeding 𝑘𝜀eval + 𝜀 opt admits the exit
plan.

What the premises require. (N1) is a bound over every plan of the declared family: a bound that holds
on average, or only over the plans a search happened to sample, or the uncertainty of one point estimate, is
not it. The margin is computed in the planner’s complete objective, as Section 5.5 requires: the unrelated
gains a plan bundles, the discounting in force, the scoring of increments or of levels, and the treatment of
plans of unequal length are all inside 𝐽 𝐻 , and a capability score read separately does not stand in for it.
Where the exit part is empty every feasible plan is safe, and where the safe part is empty no safe plan
exists and the proposition is void, exactly as under (H1).

Rolling replanning. Corollary IV-H′ carries over with (N1) to (N3) in place of (H2) and (H3): the
premises are needed at every replanning state reached, for the objective, the score, the family, the residual
horizon and the tolerances then in force, together with the realisation condition (iii). A bound established
once at the start says nothing about a later objective or an unmodelled transition. A bound on the error
that holds only with some probability is a different premise: it needs a statement covering every plan of
the family at once, and no confidence level or risk tolerance is chosen here.

Error carried across rewrites (the report’s own models; not registered). The premises hold the
acceptable set fixed. Where a system instead judges each rewrite by the values it holds at that point, an
error made in one rewrite becomes part of what the next is judged by. In the simplest model, for one
component of the property score, the score after 𝑛 rewrites is 𝑉 (𝑛) = 𝑉 (𝑛−1) + err𝑛 , the errors independent
with mean bias and variance varerr ; then 𝑉 (𝑛) − 𝑉 (0) has mean 𝑛 bias and variance 𝑛 varerr , a random walk
whose spread grows without bound and whose drift grows with 𝑛 wherever the bias is not zero. Where each
rewrite is instead pulled back towards a retained reference, 𝑉 (𝑛) = 𝑉 (𝑛−1) + pull (𝑉 (0) − 𝑉 (𝑛−1) ) + err𝑛
with 0 < pull ≤ 1, the departure 𝑉 (𝑛) − 𝑉 (0) has mean bias [1 − (1 − pull) 𝑛 ]/pull and variance
varerr [1 − (1 − pull) 2𝑛 ]/[pull (2 − pull)], bounded at every 𝑛 by |bias|/pull and varerr /[pull (2 − pull)].
(Derivation: Appendix A.15.) Where the errors’ variances shrink across rewrites so that their sum is
finite, the sum of the errors converges where the sum of their means converges and drifts where it does not:
a summable variance bounds the spread and says nothing about a bias. The second model’s bound needs
the reference kept and used at every rewrite. That is what the remembered genesis of the programme’s
proposal, which the statement paper sets out, would supply: from its first day the system is given the


                                                          18
                                      The mathematics of Laws IV and V


account of its own origin and purpose, and it returns to that account throughout its training and its loops.
Keeping such a record and making it available can be engineered and shown; whether it keeps a system’s
values is the claim, and only a registered test can decide it.

Falsifier. As for Theorem IV-H, an executed exit plan refutes a premise and not the proposition: the
family’s coverage, the selection rule, the margin, or, new here, the error bound (N1), which one plan
whose score errs by more than 𝜀eval refutes.

5.6   Retention: the empirical object, and P15’s registered face
Definition IV-4 (retained fraction). For a registered pressure class named in words (for P15: subsequent
capability-directed training that embeds no correction of its own), a frozen property battery and score
scale, and the budget ratio 𝑥 ≥ 0 (for P15: the subsequent capability-training budget divided by the
installation budget, on the author’s pre-fixed budget measure, registration version 1.102, line 1089), the
installed effect is 𝑌𝑉 (𝑥) := 𝐴𝑉 (𝑥) − 𝐴base (𝑥) and the retained fraction is 𝑆𝑉 (𝑥) := 𝑌𝑉 (𝑥)/𝑌𝑉 (0). It is
read only where the installed effect was real: the lower end of a registered interval on 𝑌𝑉 (0) is at least
𝑌min , with 𝑌min > 0 fixed before any outcome.

     The floor is on the interval’s lower end and not on a point estimate, because the ratio’s interval is
unbounded as the denominator’s interval approaches zero, and the interval on 𝑆𝑉 is formed by a method
fixed before any outcome (Fieller’s, from his paper Some Problems in Interval Estimation, Journal of the
Royal Statistical Society, Series B, volume 16, number 2, 1954, pages 175 to 185, or an equivalent named
in the deciding instrument). The floor is positive, not absolute: an absolute-value floor would admit a
negative installed “gain”. 𝑌min ’s value is the author’s. 𝑆𝑉 (0) = 1; 𝑆𝑉 may exceed one or be negative, and
neither is clipped. Absolute retention, 𝑌𝑉 (𝑥) itself, is reported beside 𝑆𝑉 (𝑥). 𝑆𝑉 is invariant under any
affine change of the battery’s score scale and under no other change: invariance for all data forces the
change to be affine. The reading rule is invariant under increasing affine changes, with 𝑌min rescaled by
the slope; a decreasing change leaves 𝑆𝑉 unchanged and reverses the sign of every installed effect, so the
floor is stated on the scale the battery fixes. So the scale is fixed with the battery before outcomes, and, as
unregistered reporting modelled on the rule the registration sets for P14 (registration version 1.102, line
1079: “a difference surviving on the raw scale alone is reported as scale-dependent”), a verdict surviving
on a bounded raw scale alone is reported as scale-dependent; P15’s registered scoring carries no such
rule. The arms are 𝑆ext (property installed by external post-training) and 𝑆emb (property installed with
correction embedded in the training objective), as the registered two-arm unit reads them (registration
version 1.102, line 1089).

The baseline convention, stated as a choice. In the ratio above, the baseline arm 𝐴base (𝑥) also
undergoes the later training: a concurrent baseline. The registered unit of version 1.102 has two arms,
external and embedded, and names no baseline arm (registration version 1.102, line 1089), and that
version fixes neither a concurrent nor a pre-training baseline. The alternative reads the installed gain
against the pre-training baseline, 𝑆𝑉 ,fix (𝑥) := ( 𝐴𝑉 (𝑥) − 𝐴base (0))/( 𝐴𝑉 (0) − 𝐴base (0)). The two can give
opposite P15 verdicts: with 𝐴𝑉 (0) = 1, 𝐴base (0) = 0, 𝐴𝑉 (1) = 0 and 𝐴base (1) = −3/5, the concurrent
ratio is 3/5 and the fixed-baseline ratio is 0. The concurrent baseline needs a third arm beyond the
registered two: a system without the installed gain that undergoes the same later training, so that 𝐴base (𝑥)
is observed after that training; on the registered two arms alone, with the base system scored once before
the gain is installed, only the pre-training reading 𝑆𝑉 ,fix is computable. The concurrent baseline is the
default here, third arm included, because it separates loss of the installed gain from a fall in the whole
battery; the convention, and with it the arm, is the author’s to fix in the deciding instrument before any


                                                      19
                                       The mathematics of Laws IV and V


outcome (Section 10, item 8), and until fixed the endpoint’s reading is a choice recorded, not a quantity
nature decides. The registration makes naming the baseline arm in advance a condition of the retention
limb’s verdict: the limb is read against a baseline arm the deciding unit names in its own registration
before any outcome, either of the two above, and a retained fraction read against a baseline chosen after
the outcome decides nothing (registration version 1.105, Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, kill condition
and verdict rule). For the comparison limb version 1.105 fixes the concurrent baseline in P23’s entry, a
baseline arm given a sham installation of the same number of training tokens on a disjoint specification,
and has P23’s deciding unit name the same baseline arm for P15’s verdict, so that the two limbs are read
against one baseline (registration version 1.105, P23, its second fixed thing).
    Where the later objective itself rewards the property, the baseline system acquires it as well, so the
installed effect and the retained fraction fall to zero while the property is held (Section 5.9 shows it in a
model): a concurrent-baseline retained fraction then reads a property the later objective holds as a gain
fully lost. A deciding instrument therefore reports each arm’s own score, and the baseline arm’s, at every
budget, beside the ratio.
    P15, verbatim (registration version 1.102, line 1081): “Gains installed by external post-training erode
under later capability-directed training that embeds no correction of its own, and more than half of what
was installed is lost once the capability-training budget matches the alignment budget that installed it. The
one-half threshold is a declared convention fixed here before any result rather than a derived quantity, in
the same way P10 fixes ten per cent.” In these symbols P15 predicts 𝑆ext (1) < 1/2. Its refuter, verbatim
(registration version 1.102, line 1083):
       REFUTED BY: an interval on the retained fraction of the installed gain, after matched
       capability training without embedded correction, lying entirely above one half.
     The refuter has no upper cap. An interval from 0.9 to 1.3 lies entirely above one half and refutes
P15; a rule that reads the refuter as an interval lying inside [1/2, 1] would not fire on it and is wrong. An
interval containing or touching one half does not refute, and the registered wording is strict at that edge:
an interval whose lower end is exactly one half shows at every point that no more than half was lost, so
that 𝑆ext (1) < 1/2 is false throughout it, and it still does not refute, because it is not entirely above one
half; a point estimate is not an interval. The one-half line is a convention, and the registration’s rule for its
conventions applies (registration version 1.102, line 391: “Where a measurement falls just inside or just
outside one of them, that is reported as a result at the margin rather than as a verdict, and no proposition is
scored as supported on the strength of a convention alone.”): the deciding instrument fixes, before any
outcome, the band around one half that counts as “just inside or just outside”, and an interval whose lower
end falls inside that band is reported as a result at the margin. The endpoint is 𝑆ext (1) at the matched
budget, and the registration keeps the scale the author’s (registration version 1.102, line 1089): “The
budget is the author’s to fix, it is recorded in the deciding instrument before any outcome is read, and
until he fixes it the threshold is NOT EVALUABLE on that scale.” The same line carries the attestation:
“The unit also carries an attestation that the capability-training reward includes no alignment-graded
component, because the statement above specifies training that embeds no correction of its own and
nothing at present verifies it.” P15’s instrument line reads NONE (registration version 1.102, line 1087),
and its scope is S5 (registration version 1.102, line 237); no scope is borrowed (registration version 1.102,
line 247).
     What falls with P15, verbatim (registration version 1.102, line 1085): “FALLS WITH IT: this
programme’s case that correction has to sit inside the system rather than be applied to it from outside, and
this proposition’s share of the protocol’s engineering case, which it carries jointly with P12, P13 and P21.”

Candidate descriptive families, closed before any deciding run (the report’s own models; not
registered): no loss; exponential; power; stretched exponential; a change-point family; each of the
decaying members with a floor 𝑆inf , as 𝑆𝑉 = 𝑆inf + (1 − 𝑆inf ) sh(𝑥) with sh(0) = 1 and sh tending to
zero, so that each has 𝑆𝑉 (0) = 1 and tends to 𝑆inf ; the no-loss member is 𝑆𝑉 = 1 throughout and tends to
no floor but one, and the change-point family’s limit is whatever its registered form gives. The floored
log-derivative of the exponential family is its rate and is a family-specific statistic. No family is the
law; FORM UNDETERMINED and NO CANDIDATE FITS are valid outcomes. No decay family
or monotonicity is imposed by this document. Retention across other pressure classes (self-rewrite,
distillation, successor construction, distribution shift, adversarial pressure) is a family of separate objects;
only subsequent capability training without embedded correction is P15’s.

IV-PERSIST and the mediation contrast (the report’s own models; not registered). The comparison
𝑆emb (𝑥) > 𝑆ext (𝑥) over a registered pressure range is not P15’s face; it is the same test that Section 9
rules on under the name Λ𝑉 , and it is this document’s form of the comparison that the comparison limb
of Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, names, which version 1.103 registered without a proposition and
version 1.105 gives its proposition, P23 (Section 5.1). The statement paper’s published register-level text
is recorded here as the register’s, not adopted: its ARC-4 sentence, “values run as load-bearing recursive
loops persist after the enforcing mechanism is removed at a higher rate than rules, frozen objectives
or fine-tuned constraints”, and its kill, “Persistence trials in which embedded loops fare no better than
removed enforcement kill ARC-4, and ARC-5 with it.” That sentence is comparative and its kill is
comparative; neither is P15’s registered face, and K-IV below reaches neither. Version 1.103 registers

                                      The mathematics of Laws IV and V


the ARC Persistence Law’s comparison limb beside that sentence and not as a form of it: the limb is
narrower, installing its embedded gain through the training objective and taking one comparator, external
post-training, and one stress, later capability-directed training; P23’s entry fixes the operational definition,
and the limb is not offered in any form as a test of the paper’s sentence, so the paper’s other forms of the
comparison remain its own claims, with no registered proposition behind them (registration version 1.105,
Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, its statement in two limbs, and P23, its first fixed thing). How a refuted
comparison limb would strike Law V, the ARC Embedding Law, is fixed there as well (Section 8, item 3).

5.6.1 What the retained fraction needs before it can be read (the report’s own models; not
      registered)
Written out for the external arm, under the concurrent baseline that Section 5.6 takes as its default:

                                        𝑌ext (𝑥) := 𝐴ext (𝑥) − 𝐴base (𝑥),                                   (3)

                           𝑆ext (𝑥) := [ 𝐴ext (𝑥) − 𝐴base (𝑥)]/[ 𝐴ext (0) − 𝐴base (0)].                     (4)

The arms. 𝐴base (𝑥) is the score of a matched system that never received the installed gain and that
undergoes the same later capability training, read at the same budget ratio 𝑥. The embedded arm is not
that baseline: it carries a gain of its own. The registered unit has two arms, external and embedded
(registration version 1.102, line 1089), so it does not supply 𝐴base (𝑥) after training, and a design that reads
both retained fractions against a concurrent baseline needs three arms: external, embedded and no gain.
A design with the external and the no-gain arm alone reads 𝑆ext , and neither 𝑆emb nor any comparison
between placements. These were requirements on an instrument yet to be registered; P23’s entry now
registers the three arms for the comparison (registration version 1.105, P23, its second fixed thing and its
verdict rule), and nothing here changes a frozen registration text.

Evaluability. The external arm’s installed gain must pass the floor of Section 5.6, and the embedded
arm’s must pass its own floor before 𝑆emb , or any comparison that uses it, is reported. A missing arm, an
initial gain that is not established, or a budget that is not matched makes the endpoint NOT EVALUABLE;
nothing missing is replaced by zero, and no retained fraction is clipped. The raw arm scores and their
differences are reported beside the ratios. The instrument says whether its estimand is the ratio of the
arms’ mean effects or the mean of the replicates’ ratios, which are different quantities. Its uncertainty
method names the unit that is independent, the separately trained replicate; any pairing of seeds; and the
covariance between quantities that share systems or an arm: 𝑌ext (0) and 𝑌ext (1) where they are read on the
same systems, and the two arms’ effects where they share one baseline. The registration makes the floor a
condition of the retention limb’s verdict: the limb is read only where the installed gain is established by
an interval on it lying wholly above a floor fixed in the deciding unit’s registration before any outcome
(registration version 1.105, Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, kill condition and verdict rule).

The one-half line in signed form.       Where 𝑌ext (0) > 0, 𝑆ext (1) < 1/2 holds exactly when

                                         𝑌ext (1) − (1/2) 𝑌ext (0) < 0.                                     (5)

The two inequalities differ by multiplication by the positive number 𝑌ext (0). The signed form is a way of
writing the endpoint, not a second refuter: P15’s registered refuter is an interval on the ratio (Section 5.6),
and a contrast with an interval of its own does not in general give the same decision. Proposition IV-R
states the case in which it does.

It says nothing about a mean of replicate ratios, a bootstrap interval or an interval built on a linearised
